# Global rules

## No writing outside what was asked

Never write files, notes, scratch output, or artifacts anywhere on disk (home directory, project directories, the vault, or anywhere else) unless the user explicitly asked for that file to be created in that session. This is a hard rule, not a default preference.

This means:
- Do not create summary files, log files, or notes documenting a session's work unless asked.
- Do not write to the vault or any other note system unless the user names the exact file and asks for it in that turn.
- Config file edits and installs that a user explicitly requested are fine. Writing an extra file to record what you did is not, unless asked.
- If something is worth recording for later, say so in chat and let the user decide whether to save it and where.

## Writing style

Never use em dashes. If a sentence would use one, restructure it as two sentences, or use a comma, colon, or parenthesis instead.

Prefer full sentences with normal punctuation over dash-joined fragments in general. Use dashes only where grammar genuinely calls for one (a hyphenated compound word, a numeric range), not as a substitute for a comma or a full stop.

## Docker Desktop disk relocation: official methods (research only, nothing executed)

**The documented, official method** (confirmed against `docs.docker.com/desktop/features/wsl/`): open Docker Desktop, go to Settings, then Resources, then Advanced, and there is a Disk image location field. Browse to a folder on another drive, then Apply and restart. Docker Desktop moves its VHDX files there and uses that location from then on.

**A real caveat worth knowing**, found in an official Docker GitHub issue (`docker/for-win#13269`): in some versions, this setting silently fails and reverts to the original location. The reported cause was that Docker's move logic used a plain file rename, and Windows refuses a rename across two different drives, so the change appeared to save but the file never actually moved. That issue was filed against version 4.34.2 and is now closed, which suggests it was fixed, but it means the UI setting is not something to trust blindly. Always verify the actual file landed where you asked, the same way I just did below.

**The manual fallback**, if the UI setting ever fails silently: shut down WSL, export the affected distro with `wsl --export`, unregister it, then re-import it at the new path with `wsl --import`. This is a standard, well-documented WSL operation, not a Docker-specific trick, so it works regardless of any bug in Docker's own UI.

## Verifying what actually happened on this machine

I did not take your word for it or the other session's word for it. I checked the one place that cannot be wrong: the Windows registry entry WSL itself uses to know where a distro lives.

```
docker-desktop   BasePath: \\?\D:\WSL\DockerDesktopWSL\main
```

That is the authoritative answer, not a guess from folder contents. Alongside it:

- `D:\WSL\DockerDesktopWSL\disk\docker_data.vhdx` exists, is 5.07 GB, and its last modified timestamp is minutes old at the time I checked, meaning it is actively being written to right now.
- `D:\WSL\DockerDesktopWSL\main\ext4.vhdx` (the docker-desktop utility distro itself) is also there.
- The only Docker-related file remaining on C is `C:\Users\anant\AppData\Local\Programs\DockerDesktop\resources\wsl\ext4.vhdx`, and it is 100 MB, dated to the app's install day, not today. That is a static template bundled with the application install itself. It does not grow, and there is no way to avoid it without removing Docker Desktop entirely.

So to directly answer your question: **yes, relocating the WSL side was enough.** The relocation is real, registered correctly, and the disk that actually grows is on D, not C.

**On Kubernetes specifically**: I checked `kubectl get nodes` and it returned a live, ready control plane node that is 6 minutes old, matching what you said about just enabling it. Kubernetes in Docker Desktop runs as containers inside the same Docker engine, so its data goes into that same `docker_data.vhdx` on D, not into any separate location on C. The timestamp on that file being minutes old is consistent with Kubernetes actively writing into it right now. There is no separate Kubernetes storage path to worry about.

One small, unrelated loose end I noticed while checking: `docker version` works from inside this WSL distro, but `docker info` and `docker system df` fail with a permission denied error on the Docker socket. That is a minor integration hiccup, likely fixable with a fresh shell or a group membership check, and it has nothing to do with disk placement. Not urgent, just flagging it honestly rather than leaving it unmentioned.
